more than 10 GWh from 31 December 2002; -	in the Flemish Region:	more than 1 GWh
more than 56 Kva from 1 March 2003
-	in the Walloon Region:	greater than or equal to 20 GWh
greater than or equal to 10 GWh by 31 December 2002. -	in the Brussels-Capital Region:   more than 20 GWh
more than 10 GWh from 01 January 2003. 6.5. Barriers to entry
According to the notifying parties, entry into the market is, in terms of costs, relatively easy since it is sufficient to establish a purchase/sale office. In practice, this appears much less obvious. Indeed, the examination of the file and the hearings have revealed in particular the existence of legal and technical barriers to entry into the Belgian market. 6.5.1. Legal barriers
6.5.1.1. Legal uncertainties
It must be noted that the legislation, which in practice enshrines the right of the eligible customer to obtain supplies from the supplier of its choice, is still incomplete. Certain regulations aimed at ensuring transparent and non-discriminatory access to the network are still missing. This legal vacuum has the effect of de facto favouring the incumbent operator. 6.5.1.2. Duration of supply contracts/Termination
A transitional system has been put in place in the Walloon Region for (becoming) eligible customers. According to this system, the distribution network operator, being unable to engage in activities of supplying eligible customers, designates a supplier for this purpose (in this case Electrabel). The intercommunal company, two months before the eligibility date, has the obligation to inform the final customer thereof. Once it has become eligible, the customer continues to be supplied by the supplier designated by the operator, in the absence of an agreement entered into with another supplier. However, it remains free to change supplier subject to one month's notice. The freedom to change supplier and, consequently, to terminate the supply by the designated supplier is only complete in the absence of a contract duly signed with the designated supplier. It emerges from the examination and the hearings that a further examination is necessary regarding the duration of these contracts. 6.5.2. Technical/physical barriers: interconnection
The importation of electricity from abroad is substantially restricted by the lack of interconnection capacity at the borders. Belgium has interconnection lines with France and the Netherlands. Indeed, in its decision in case IV/M.1803: Electrabel/Epon, the Commission indicates that:
"Concerning Belgium, there are also restrictions at the interconnection level: it is noted that theoretical imports from the Netherlands to Belgium represent a negligible quantity."
Not only are interconnection capacities limited, but moreover they are not entirely allocated according to market rules. Indeed, despite an auction system put in place in 2001, it should be emphasised that interconnection capacities are still reserved for Electrabel. The same problem exists at the Franco-Belgian border, especially since the demand for interconnections is greater in the France-Belgium direction. 6.6. Strengthening of Electrabel's dominant position
The envisaged operation could be such as to render the market impenetrable for too long a period. Indeed, due to Electrabel's privileged position, the latter already benefits from the aforementioned competitive advantages. It moreover has access to essential information which competitors do not have or have only in a fragmentary manner. Examples include the conditions of access to and use of the network and the list of eligible customers and those who will become eligible. An illustration of Electrabel's use of this privileged information is found in its 2001 annual report, where Electrabel specifies that it deployed in 2001 a very targeted marketing action towards the approximately 2000 customers eligible in Flanders from 1 January 2002. Any barriers must be examined all the more strictly as the market is in full transition phase and it is a matter of avoiding the establishment of a de facto situation damaging to the development of genuine competition, before all the regulatory mechanisms (and in particular regulatory authorities having the fullness of their powers) function effectively. 7. Reaction of the companies questioned
All the competing companies that responded expressed at the very least clear reservations or even an unfavourable opinion regarding the present operation. They confirm the existence of legal and technical barriers to access to the Belgian market and claim to experience significant difficulties in proposing competitive contracts. As EDF notes in its response of 10 June 2002:
"In view of the foregoing, it is therefore possible to consider that the transfer to a single operator of all the eligible clientele of the intercommunal companies could be justified by considerations relating to the necessary reorganisation of the management and distribution of electricity in Belgium under conditions provided for by the texts. However, it would be different if this transfer were to take place immediately in favour of a single operator, without the uncertainties relating in particular to the identification of eligible customers, as well as to the intermediate costs of distribution and transmission of energy, which constitute significant barriers for competitors of the incumbent operator wishing to access the Belgian market, having been removed. In order to promote fair competition between the incumbent operator and the new players, for the benefit of final consumers, it would therefore be necessary beforehand and as a priority to establish and implement clear, transparent and non-discriminatory rules concerning the regimes of access to the transmission and distribution networks for electricity to eligible Belgian customers, as well as the conditions for supplying this electricity in Belgium (regulation, information of eligible customers about suppliers operating in the Belgian market, ease of termination of contracts with the incumbent operator, in particular)."
CREG also emphasised the need to carry out a further examination of the contractual relations that will be established between ECS and eligible customers. 8. Conclusion
The elements gathered during the examination carried out by the Service and during the hearings show that at this stage of the procedure, the notified concentration raises serious doubts as to its compatibility and that it is necessary to initiate the procedure provided for in Article 34 of the L.P.C.E. FOR THESE REASONS
The Competition Council
—	Notes that the operation in question falls within the scope of the law of 5 August 1991 on the protection of economic competition and that it raises, in accordance with Article 33, § 2, b), serious doubts as to its compatibility; —	Decides to initiate the procedure provided for in Article 34 and that it is appropriate to examine in particular:
—	The procedures for making available information relating to customers who have become eligible to market players; —	The contractual conditions proposed by ECS; —	Any conditions for compatibility of the operation; —	The real capacity of competitors to offer another solution than that envisaged by the operation. —	Invites the body of rapporteurs to communicate to it a supplementary reasoned report no later than 31 July 2002; Thus decided on 28 June 2002 by the chamber of the Competition Council composed of Mr Patrick De Wolf, chamber president, Mrs Marie-Claude Grégoire and Dominique Smeets and Mr Pierre Battard, members. FEDERAL PUBLIC SERVICE ECONOMY, SMEs, MIDDLE CLASSES AND ENERGY
[C − 2002/11454]
Competition Council. — Decision No. 2002-C/C-50 of 28 June 2002
Case CONC-C/C-02/25: ELECTRABEL CUSTOMER SOLUTIONS S.A./INTERLUX S.C.R.L. In the matter of:
Electrabel Customer Solutions S.A. (hereinafter "ECS"), having its registered office at boulevard du Régent 8, 1000 Brussels
and
Intercommunale pour la distribution d'énergie pour la province du Luxembourg having taken the form of an S.C.R.L. (hereinafter Interlux), having its registered office at the town hall of Arlon, rue Paul Reuter 8, 6700 Arlon; Having regard to the law of 5 August 1991 on the protection of economic competition coordinated on 1 July 1999 (hereinafter LPCE); Having regard to the notification dated 25 April 2002 of a concentration which consists of the acquisition of exclusive control by ECS, a subsidiary of Electrabel, of Interlux's activity of supplying electricity to its customers as they become eligible; Having regard to the documents in the file of the Competition Service; Having regard to the reasoned report of the Body of Rapporteurs dated 11 June 2002; Having regard to the decision on the confidentiality of the documents in the file of 13 June 2002; Having regard to the request submitted on the basis of Article 32quater, § 2 LPCE, on 13 June 2002 by S.A. Luminus to be heard in this procedure; Having regard to the decision of 18 June 2002 granting this request; Having regard to the request dated 20 June 2002 and transmitted to the Competition Council on 21 June 2002 by which the Commission for Electricity and Gas Regulation (abbreviated CREG) requests, on the basis of Article 32quater, § 2 LPCE and Article 23 of the law of 29 April 1999 on the organisation of the electricity market, to be heard in this procedure; Having regard to the decision of 24 June 2002 granting this request; Having heard at the hearing of 26 June 2002, S.A. Luminus and the Commission for Electricity and Gas Regulation; Having heard at the hearing of 27 June 2002 Mr rapporteur P. Marchand and the notifying parties represented by
Mr de Garcia and Mr Baeten and assisted by Mr Vandencasteele, Mr Brinckman and Mr Vronincks, lawyers in Brussels; 1. the notifying parties
1.1. Acquirer
The company Electrabel Customer Solutions (hereinafter "ECS") is a public limited company under Belgian law incorporated by deed of 12 December 2001 published in the annexes to the Moniteur belge of 28 December 2001, having its registered office at 1000 Brussels, boulevard du Régent 8, and having as its object the supply of electricity and gas as well as the supply of products and services related thereto. This company is in fact a 100% subsidiary of S.A. Electrabel. The public limited company under Belgian law Electrabel having its registered office at 1000 Brussels, boulevard du Régent 8, is a company active at all stages in the electricity sector, from production to supply to the final customer. 1.2. Seller
The Intercommunale pour la distribution d'énergie pour la province du Luxembourg (hereinafter "Interlux") is an intercommunal association having taken the form of a cooperative company with limited liability, established to ensure the distribution of energy in the province of Luxembourg. Its main activity is the distribution of electricity and gas to customers located in the territory of the affiliated municipalities. Its registered office is established at the town hall of Arlon, rue Paul Reuter 8. Interlux is a mixed intercommunal company. It is established on the basis of a partnership with a private company, in this case Electrabel, currently the majority shareholder (to the extent of 69%). The municipalities that are associated with it hold 31% of the capital. Regulatory provisions and in particular the provisions of the decree of 5 December 1996 on Walloon intercommunal companies, however provide that the municipalities always have the majority of votes as well as the chairmanship in the management and control bodies, regardless of the proportion of the contributions of the various parties to the formation of the capital. 1.3. Target undertaking or part of undertaking
The operation targets Interlux's clientele becoming eligible in the electricity supply market. 2. Description of the operation
2.1. Preamble
The notified concentration concerns the electricity market which is currently in full liberalisation phase. This liberalisation of the electricity market was decided at European level by Directive 96/92 of 19 December 1996 which imposes a limited and progressive opening of the electricity market. This liberalisation is accompanied by regulation which pays particular attention to access to the network. Energy in Belgium is a matter whose powers are divided between the federal authority and the Regions. In a very schematic manner, this distribution of powers is established as follows:
—	the federal State is competent with regard to production, transmission and tariffs; —	distribution and supply are regional matters. The normative framework in this matter includes:
—	the law of 29 April 1999 on the organisation of the electricity market (hereinafter "electricity law") published in the Moniteur belge of 11 May 1999; —	the decree of the Flemish Region of 17 July 2000 on the organisation of the electricity market (hereinafter
"Flemish decree"); —	the decree of the Walloon Region of 12 April 2001 on the organisation of the regional electricity market (hereinafter "Walloon decree") and the order of the Walloon Government of 21 March 2002 on customers who have become eligible and the control of their eligibility; —	the ordinance of 19 July 2001 on the organisation of the electricity market in the Brussels-Capital Region (hereinafter "the ordinance"). The different levels of competence have adopted their own market opening timetables. These differ both in terms of eligibility thresholds and dates. At the end of this liberalisation of the electricity market in Belgium, the distribution of electricity should be split into two activities to be entrusted to separate companies:
—	network management, which concerns the operation, maintenance and development of the distribution network. The network operator must concentrate on its main activity. It may not carry out production activities - other than green electricity - nor sales activities - other than sales to captive clientele and sales to so-called protected clientele. —	the sale of electricity which concerns the supply of energy to eligible customers. However, as early as 1996, municipalities in partnership with Electrabel had decided to pursue, within mixed intercommunal companies, activities of managing the distribution network and selling to clientele under municipal monopoly. Mixed intercommunal companies thus currently ensure the management of the distribution network as well as the supply of electricity. However, Article 8, § 1, of the Walloon decree on the organisation of the regional electricity market applicable to Interlux expressly provides that the distribution network operator may not carry out production activities other than green electricity or electricity sales other than sales necessitated by its activity as network operator. The distribution network operator may not engage in activities of supplying eligible customers nor in the supply of other services on the electricity market which are not directly related to the performance of the tasks referred to in Article 11 (i.e. network management). However, at the request of the municipalities, the distribution network operator may supply electricity to captive customers... It follows that Walloon mixed intercommunal companies, such as Interlux, which wish to be designated as network operator, will no longer be able to supply electricity to (become) eligible customers at the same time. Given the implications of the ongoing liberalisation process, Electrabel and Intermixt Wallonie (public utility establishment bringing together all the public representatives of the mixed intercommunal companies) have thus negotiated the terms of a new agreement capable of maintaining their partnership in a balanced framework. The principle adopted is that the municipalities and the intercommunal companies will become more involved in network management, monopolistic and regulated type activities, while Electrabel will concentrate primarily on the activity of supplying customers. This specialisation of each is not exclusive: the municipalities via the intercommunal companies will continue to have an interest in the result of the electricity marketing company and Electrabel will retain a participation - minority but significant - in the capital of the distribution network operators whose operation it will continue to ensure. In order to valorise the clientele that these intercommunal companies currently supply with electricity and which they will no longer be able to supply upon their eligibility, the municipalities have agreed with their partner within the mixed intercommunal companies, Electrabel, that a company ECS, incorporated in December 2001 and whose shares are entirely held by Electrabel, will take over this clientele. In consideration for this acquisition of control by ECS and this transfer of clientele by Interlux, the municipalities are offered the possibility of increasing their shareholding in the intercommunal company. These municipalities will moreover become minority shareholders (maximum 5% of the capital) in this company ECS and will participate in the profits to the extent of 40% of the result achieved. These negotiations resulted in the preparation of various agreements and a Memorandum of Understanding signed on 30 March 2001. 2.2. Operation covered by the notification
The operation which is the subject of this notification consists of the takeover by ECS (incorporated on 12 December 2001 and a 100% subsidiary of Electrabel), of Interlux's clientele concerning the supply of electricity to its customers as they become eligible and insofar as they do not decide to conclude a contract with another supplier. An eligible customer means the customer who has the right to conclude a supply contract with the company of its choice and for this purpose has a right of access to the network. The takeover by ECS of this eligible clientele of Interlux covered by this notification was not the subject of a specific document but must be deduced from a set of documents including the document entitled "Memorandum of understanding between Intermix-Electrabel" signed on 30 March 2001 and the amendments made to the articles of association of Interlux adopted on 25 March 2002. 3. Time limits
The notification made on 25 April 2002 was incomplete on an important point within the meaning of Article 5, § 2, of the Royal Decree of 23 March 1993 on the notification of concentrations of undertakings: it did not contain in particular the agreement constituting the notified concentration, nor the minutes of the General Meeting of 25 March 2002 amending the articles of association of Interlux. The missing information was provided by the parties to the rapporteur on 13 May 2002. The time limit referred to in Article 33 of the law therefore begins to run on 14 May 2002 and expires in application of Article 33, §§ 1 and 2 of the LPCE, on 28 June 2002. 4. Scope of application
The companies ECS and Interlux are undertakings within the meaning of Article 1 of the LPCE. After the concentration, ECS will hold exclusive control of Interlux's activity of supplying electricity to eligible customers (or those who will become eligible). The operation therefore constitutes a concentration within the meaning of Article 9, § 1, of the law. On the basis of the figures provided by the parties in the notification, the turnover thresholds referred to in Article 11 of the law are met. 5. Relevant market
5.1. Relevant economic sector
The economic sector concerned by the concentration is that of the production and distribution of electricity (NACE code: 40.1). 5.2. Relevant product market
In the electricity sector, five different types of activities can be distinguished, namely generation, transmission, distribution, supply and trading. —	Generation consists of the production of electricity. —	Transmission is the conveyance of electricity over high-voltage cables. —	Distribution is the conveyance of electricity over medium and low-voltage cables. —	Supply consists of delivery to the final consumer. —	Trading is the purchase and resale of electricity. The first four activities can be considered as belonging to distinct product markets, since they require different assets and resources and because the market and competitive conditions are different for each of them. As regards electricity trading, the question of whether it constitutes a market distinct from the general trading of energy products has been left open by the European Commission. Since this market does not constitute a relevant market for the present case, there is no need to rule on this question. Supply of electricity
Following the liberalisation of part of the electricity market initiated by Directive EC 96/92, a further distinction must be made between the supply of electricity to eligible and non-eligible customers (or captive customers, i.e. those who cannot choose their supplier). These belong to two distinct product markets, insofar as the competitive conditions are different, and where, most often, they are subject to specific regulations. It is in this context that the present operation takes place since, pursuant to Article 8, § 1, of the Walloon decree, the distribution network operator is prohibited from any activity of supplying eligible customers. This network operator will probably be the intercommunal company or a new subsidiary thereof created for this purpose. Electrabel is active directly or indirectly in the markets for production, transmission, distribution, supply of electricity and trading of products related to electricity. Interlux is active in the markets for distribution and supply of electricity. The latter activity, insofar as it concerns customers becoming eligible who have not chosen a supplier, will be carried out by ECS, a 100% subsidiary of Electrabel. Consequently, the relevant product market is that of the supply of electricity to eligible customers. 5.3. Relevant geographic market
The geographic market is at most national. Indeed, given the problems related to the limited capacities of interconnections, imports are restricted to a fairly significant extent. 6. Competitive analysis
6.1. Structure of supply: dominant position of Electrabel
Electrabel occupies a privileged position in the Belgian market since it holds extremely high market shares regardless of the market considered. 6.1.1. Supply
The market for the supply of electricity to eligible customers in Belgium is estimated by the parties in volume (GWh) at 30,962.4 for the year 2001. Electrabel's market share amounts to more than 90%. No other competitor, whatever its economic and financial power (EDF, RWE, E.ON), individually holds a market share greater than or equal to 5%. 6.1.2. Production
Electrabel's dominant position in the market for supply to eligible customers must also be examined taking into account Electrabel's quasi-monopolistic situation in the production market in Belgium. Since 1995, S.A. Electrabel and S.A. SPE have been grouped within a cooperative company (CPTE) whose purpose is to ensure the coordination of the production and transmission of electrical energy of the two companies. Electrabel is a shareholder therein to the extent of 91.5%.Out of a total production in 2001 estimated at 75,953 GWh by the CREG and at (trade secrets) GWh by the notifying parties, (trade secrets) GWh were produced by Electrabel, representing a market share of (more than 90% - the exact percentage is a trade secret). No significant evolution is to be expected in this market, either in the short or medium term. It would even tend to strengthen Electrabel's position. This situation is all the more problematic given that electricity imports are at a fairly low level. 6.2. Transmission
The transmission activity concerns the conveyance of electricity on the high-voltage network. The cooperative company CPTE, a subsidiary of Electrabel and SPE, owned the major part of the high-voltage network in Belgium and managed it. On 28 June 2001, the high-voltage network was transferred to a subsidiary company of CPTE, the company ELIA. The latter will be the transmission system operator whose designation, tasks and control are regulated by the electricity law and the implementing decrees. 6.3. Distribution
Electrabel also has a privileged position at the distribution level (conveyance of electricity from the transmission network to the consumer on a medium and low-voltage network). Indeed, distribution activities fall under the monopoly of municipalities, most of which have joined together within inter-municipal companies. These inter-municipal companies are for the most part qualified as mixed because they include a private partner, in this case in all cases Electrabel, which is generally a majority shareholder. Thus, the 16 mixed inter-municipal companies in which Electrabel is a shareholder bring together more than 500 municipalities and serve more than 8 million inhabitants in Belgium daily, representing more than 82% of the electricity distributed. 6.4. Structure of demand
Currently, the eligibility thresholds in relation to annual consumption level are as follows:
—	at federal level: more than 20 GWh
more than 10 GWh from 31 December 2002; —	in the Flemish Region: more than 1 GWh more than 56 Kva from 1 March 2003; —	in the Walloon Region: greater than or equal to 20 GWh greater than or equal to 10 GWh by 31 December 2002; —	in the Brussels-Capital Region: more than 20 GWh more than 10 GWh from 1 January 2003. 6.5. Barriers to entry
According to the notifying parties, entry into the market is, in terms of costs, relatively easy since it is sufficient to establish a purchase/sales office. In practice, this appears much less obvious. Indeed, the examination of the file and the hearings have revealed in particular the existence of legal and technical barriers to entry into the Belgian market. 6.5.1. Legal barriers
6.5.1.1. Legal uncertainties
It must be noted that the legislation, which in practice enshrines the right of the eligible customer to obtain supplies from the supplier of their choice, is still incomplete. Certain regulations aimed at ensuring transparent and non-discriminatory access to the network are still missing. This legal vacuum has the effect of de facto favouring the incumbent operator. 6.5.1.2. Duration of supply contracts/Termination
A transitional system has been put in place in the Walloon Region for (becoming) eligible customers. According to this system, the distribution network operator, being unable to engage in supply activities to eligible customers, designates a supplier for this purpose (in this case Electrabel). The inter-municipal company, two months before the eligibility date, has the obligation to inform the final customer. Once eligible, the customer continues to be supplied by the supplier designated by the operator, in the absence of an agreement concluded with another supplier. However, they remain free to change supplier subject to one month's notice. The freedom to change supplier and, consequently, to terminate the supply by the designated supplier is only complete in the absence of a duly signed contract with the designated supplier. It emerges from the examination and the hearings that a further examination is necessary regarding the duration of these contracts. 6.5.2. Technical/physical barriers: interconnection
The importation of electricity from abroad is substantially restricted by the lack of interconnection capacity at the borders. Belgium has interconnection lines with France and the Netherlands. Indeed, in its decision in case IV/M.1803: Electrabel/Epon, the Commission indicates that:
"Concerning Belgium, there are also restrictions at the interconnection level: it is noted that theoretical imports from the Netherlands to Belgium represent a negligible quantity."
Not only are interconnection capacities limited, but moreover they are not entirely allocated according to market rules. Indeed, despite an auction system put in place in 2001, it should be emphasised that interconnection capacities are still reserved for Electrabel. The same problem exists at the Franco-Belgian border, especially since the demand for interconnections is greater in the France-Belgium direction. 6.6. Strengthening of Electrabel's dominant position
The proposed transaction could be such as to render the market impenetrable for too long a period. Indeed, due to Electrabel's privileged position, the latter already benefits from the aforementioned competitive advantages. It also has access to essential information which competitors do not have or have only in a fragmentary manner. One can thus cite among others the conditions of access to and use of the network and the list of eligible customers and those who will become eligible. An illustration of Electrabel's use of this privileged information is found in its 2001 annual report, where Electrabel specifies having deployed in 2001 a very targeted marketing action towards the approximately 2000 eligible customers in Flanders from 1 January 2002. The possible barriers must be examined all the more strictly as the market is in full transition phase and it is a matter of avoiding the establishment of a de facto situation damaging to the development of genuine competition, before all the regulatory mechanisms (and in particular regulatory authorities having the full extent of their powers) function effectively. 7. Reaction of the companies questioned
All the competing companies that responded expressed at the very least clear reservations or even an unfavourable opinion regarding the present transaction. They confirm the existence of legal and technical barriers to access to the Belgian market and claim to experience significant difficulties in proposing competitive contracts. As EDF notes in its response of 10 June 2002:
"In view of the foregoing, it is therefore possible to consider that the transfer to a single operator of all the eligible customers of the inter-municipal companies could be justified by considerations relating to the necessary reorganisation of the management and distribution of electricity in Belgium under conditions provided for by the texts. However, it would be different if this transfer were to take place immediately in favour of a single operator, without the uncertainties relating in particular to the identification of eligible customers, as well as to the intermediate costs of distribution and transport of energy, which constitute significant barriers for competitors of the incumbent operator wishing to access the Belgian market, having been removed. In order to promote fair competition between the incumbent operator and new entrants, for the benefit of final consumers, it would therefore be necessary beforehand and as a priority to establish and implement clear, transparent and non-discriminatory rules concerning the regimes for access to electricity transmission and distribution networks for eligible Belgian customers, as well as the conditions for supplying this electricity in Belgium (regulation, information of eligible customers about suppliers operating in the Belgian market, ease of terminating contracts with the incumbent operator, in particular)."
The CREG also insisted on the need to carry out a further examination of the contractual relationships that will be established between ECS and eligible customers. 8. Conclusion
The elements gathered during the examination carried out by the Service and during the hearings reveal that at this stage of the procedure, the notified concentration raises serious doubts as to its admissibility and that it is necessary to initiate the procedure provided for in Article 34 of the L.P.C.E. FOR THESE REASONS
The Competition Council
—	Notes that the transaction in question falls within the scope of the law of 5 August 1991 on the protection of economic competition and that it raises, in accordance with Article 33, § 2, b), serious doubts as to its admissibility; —	Decides to initiate the procedure provided for in Article 34 and that it is appropriate to examine in particular:
—	The procedures for making information relating to customers who have become eligible available to market players; —	The contractual conditions proposed by ECS; —	The possible conditions for admissibility of the transaction; —	The real capacity of competitors to offer another solution than that envisaged by the transaction. —	Invites the body of rapporteurs to communicate to it a further reasoned report no later than 31 July 2002; Thus decided on 28 June 2002 by the chamber of the Competition Council composed of Mr Patrick De Wolf, chamber president, Mrs Marie-Claude Grégoire and Dominique Smeets and Mr Pierre Battard, members. *
FEDERAL PUBLIC SERVICE ECONOMY, SMEs, MIDDLE CLASSES AND ENERGY
[C − 2002/11451]
Competition Council. — Decision No. 2002-E/A-47 of 27 June 2002
In the matter of:
—	the French public limited company Club Méditerranée (hereinafter Club Med) whose registered office is located at 11 rue de Cambrai, 75019 Paris. Having regard to the notification of the standard contract establishing a new distribution network for Club Med products characterised by territorial exclusivity, received on 28 February 2002 and registered with the Competition Council under number CONC-E/A-01/0014 by which the company Club Med requests:
—	as a principal claim the benefit of the grant of a negative clearance provided for in Article 6 of the law of 5 August 1991 on the protection of economic competition (hereinafter referred to as LPCE); —	as a subsidiary claim, the filing of the case on the basis of Article 32 of that law; —	as a very subsidiary claim, the grant of an individual exemption under Article 2, § 3, of that law; Having regard to the reasoned report of the body of rapporteurs of 4 February 2002, notified to the company Club Med on the same day; Having heard at the hearing of 25 March 2002, the body of rapporteurs and the notifying party, the company Club Med,
represented by Mr Jeff Van Bever, General Director, and Mrs Maria Pia Nardone, assisted by their counsel, Mr Peter Wytinck, lawyer in Brussels; Whereas the composition of the chamber had to be modified for reasons of force majeure; Having heard at the hearing of 27 June 2002, the body of rapporteurs and the notifying party, the company Club Med, represented by Mr Jeff Van Bever, General Director, and Mrs Maria Pia Nardone, assisted by their counsel, Mr Peter Wytinck, lawyer in Brussels; Whereas the company Club Med carries on the activity of a general tour operator which consists essentially in the design and marketing, in nearly 50 countries, of a category of holidays based on the "all-inclusive system", including transport, accommodation, catering and entertainment. That according to the notifying party, the distribution network put in place is based on a standard exclusive agent contract. This contract will have the nature of an agency contract insofar as the final customers will be contractually bound to Club Med and not to the agencies. That in order to ensure coverage of the entire Belgian territory, in each district or municipality (depending on population density or economic potential) Club Med products will be marketed by one or more agencies, chosen according to Club Med's commercial interests and their capacity to market these products. The selected agencies will benefit from territorial exclusivity. That the contract is concluded for a period of three years, that is to say from 1 January 2002 to 31 December 2004. Admissibility of the application
Whereas the application for negative clearance and exemption submitted by the company Club Med must be declared admissible given that it is a legal person pursuing an economic objective on a lasting basis (and is therefore an undertaking within the meaning of Article 1 of the law) and that it demonstrates a sufficient interest by acting with a view to obtaining guarantees on the validity of its standard distribution contract with regard to the law. Reasons invoked by the notifying party to justify the choice of a closed distribution network
Whereas the notifying party states that its commercial policy is to ensure not only a constant improvement in the quality of its final products but also to offer, before any sale, quality advisory services to customers. That to do this, it considers that the competence of its staff is essential, and that this requires ongoing training. This requires significant investments in terms of financial and personal resources both on its part and on that of its local agencies. It maintains that this training is only possible if the distribution network is limited and claims that otherwise, the additional investments could only be reflected in the commitments it requires from accredited agencies, with the consequence of a price increase demanded from customers. That moreover, it also maintains that open distribution would jeopardise the agencies' willingness to invest in the sale of Club Med products because profitability would be called into question. That according to the company Club Med, the distribution network as envisaged will enable agencies to respond in a comprehensive, adequate and rapid manner to the demand for its products and in a profitable manner for them. It is thus justified according to it, to opt for a closed distribution network for its products. The market
The product or service market concerned
Whereas the company Club Med proposes to retain as the relevant market, the market for package holidays abroad departing from Belgium. Whereas the body of rapporteurs considers that Club Med products do indeed fall within the definition of package tours and that it might be possible in this case to envisage a segmentation of the package tour market according to the holiday formula (holidays in club village formula or others...), especially since Club Méditerranée insists on the specific characteristics of Club Med products to justify the closed nature of its distribution network. That however, the body of rapporteurs notes that the reactions of the competitors of the company Club Méditerranée on the segmentation to be retained do not go in this direction. Tour operators such as Ticket BBL Travel, VIP Travel, Sunsnacks (Thomas Cook), VTB Reizen agree with Club Méditerranée's presentation. That only Nouvelles Frontières segments its activity according to three segments which correspond to the segmentation carried out in its brochure. That in its decision Preussag/Nouvelles Frontières of 14 November 2000 (No. COMP/M.2186), the Commission considered Club Med as a competitor of Bosphorus, Nouvelles Frontières etc... on the package holiday market in Belgium, with a market share of 3-4%. That in conclusion, the Rapporteur proposes to retain as the relevant market, like the notifying party, the market for package holidays abroad departing from Belgium. Whereas the notifying company also refers to the decision of the European Commission of 22 September 1999 rendered in case IV/M.1524 Airtours/First Choice (OJ L-93 of 13 April 2000) to consider that the definition of the relevant market to be retained as regards it set out in the judgment of the Brussels Court of Appeal of 22 April 1999 was not well-founded. Whereas since then the Court of First Instance of the European Communities ruled on this decision by its judgment of 6 June 2002 (T342/99). Whereas the product market in question includes all products and/or services that the consumer considers interchangeable or substitutable by reason of their characteristics, their price and the use for which they are intended (Commission communication, OJ 1997 C/372, p 51). Whereas the Competition Council has also already ruled in concentration cases concerning this sector (decision No. 2001-C/C-32 of 18 June 2001, Ticket BBL Travel/Reizen Theo Huybrechts/Kroon Reizen/L'Epervier, decision No. 2001-C/C-48 of 19 September 2001, C & N Touristic Belgium N.V./City Bird Holding S.A./City Bird S.A. and decision No. 2002-C/C-22 of 20 February 2002, American Express International Inc/BBL Travel American Express S.A.). That the aforementioned decisions all concern concentrations. That it should be noted that the present procedure concerns a matter referred to in Article 2 L.P.C.E. Whereas the Competition Council considers, in this case and on the basis of the elements in its possession, that it cannot necessarily retain the market for package holidays abroad departing from Belgium, as the market in question in the present case. That indeed, as the company Club Med itself points out, to justify the closed nature of its distribution network, the product it offers is a specific product based on "all-inclusive" and including in the package price transport, accommodation, catering and entertainment. That moreover, the Brussels Court of Appeal, by its judgment of 22 September 1999, noted several particular and characteristic elements which would demonstrate that it is a specific market, and in particular the particular service offered, the formula of holiday villages offering particular entertainment. Whereas consequently, it is necessary to invite the body of rapporteurs to define the relevant market taking into account the degree of substitutability of the services offered, both by S.A. Club Med and by its competitors, and moreover, to assess the market share of the notifying party, on this market thus defined. Analysis of the contract
Whereas in the notification, the company Club Med. transmitted a standard contract model. Whereas this standard contract has been used since 1 January 2002; That it is appropriate to examine the contracts already entered into in order to be better able to rule on the application made by the notifying company; That the scope that is or will be given to certain clauses of this contract, also proves important; That at the hearing of 27 June 2002, a copy of a contract has already been filed; That it is important to have communicated a copy of all contracts entered into force since 1 January 2002 and covered by the present notification, in particular because of the clauses left to the parties' discretion; FOR THESE REASONS,
the Competition Council
—	decides that it is necessary to invite the body of rapporteurs to define the relevant market taking into account the degree of substitutability of the services offered, both by S.A. Club Med and by its competitors, and moreover, to assess the market share of the notifying party, on this market thus defined. —	Orders the notifying company to communicate to the body of rapporteurs a copy of all exclusive agent contracts (and amendments) in force since 1 January 2002. Thus decided on 27 June 2002 by the chamber of the Competition Council composed of Mr Patrick De Wolf, chamber president, Mrs Béatrice Ponet and Dominique Smeets, Mr Eric Balate, members. *
FEDERAL PUBLIC SERVICE ECONOMY, SMEs, MIDDLE CLASSES AND ENERGY
[C − 2002/11432]
Competition Council. — Decision No. 2002-P/K-29 of 9 April 2002
Case CONC-I/O-97/0016 In the matter of:
Stora Enso Langerbrugge, (hereinafter referred to as the complainant) a public limited company under Belgian law having its registered office at Wondelgemkaai 200, 9000 Ghent, is a subsidiary of the Stora group which also includes Stora Corbehem s.a., a company under French law whose registered office is located at Rue de Brebières in Corbehem (France). Against
Fruytier Scierie, a public limited company under Belgian law having its registered office at 3 rue Saint-Isidore, 6900 Marche-en Famenne, active in forestry operations and the sawing of softwood. Having regard to the law on the protection of economic competition, coordinated on 1 July 1999; Having regard to the complaint filed on 25 August 1997 by the complainant with the Competition Service and registered on the same day under number CONC-I/O-97/0016; Having regard to the report of the Body of rapporteurs of 24 January 2002; Having heard at the hearing of 9 April 2002, Mrs Anne Bouillet, for the body of rapporteurs, and the complainant, represented by its counsel Miguel Troncoso-Ferrer and Hugo Keulers. Whereas the present procedure was initiated on 25 August 1997 pursuant to the law of 5 August 1991 on the protection of economic competition (hereinafter L.P.C.E.), which was amended by two laws of 26 April 1999 then coordinated by royal decree on 1 July 1999; Whereas Article 47 of the law of 26 April 1999 amending the law of 5 August 1991 on the protection of economic competition states that the provisions of the new law do not apply to procedures initiated with the Competition Council or the Brussels Court of Appeal at the time of entry into force of this law, namely 1 October 1999; Whereas the text concerning prescription has not been modified; Whereas the Competition Council considers that a procedure is pending before it from the moment it has been seized by the report of the Body of rapporteurs; Whereas in this case, the report of the Body of rapporteurs having been transmitted to the Competition Council on 24 January 2002, the amended law is applicable; Whereas the complaint concerned a contract signed between the parties on 14 September 1995, a contract which the complainant considered restrictive of competition. Whereas the product market concerned is that of white TMP (thermo mechanical pulp) chips from the sawing of spruce; That these chips form the raw material necessary for the production of a certain type of quality paper manufactured by Stora Langerbrugge, SC (super-calendered mechanical printing paper) paper used for printing magazines; That there is no product truly substitutable for white TMP chips for the production of SC paper. Whereas the geographical market concerned must be delimited to the entire Belgian territory, Luxembourg, part of western Germany and eastern France, that is within a radius of 300 km around the sawmill, and this because of the high transport cost of white TMP chips. Whereas on 17 September 1997, the complainant's representatives informed the Service by letter of the withdrawal of the complaint, the contract concerned no longer being in force; That consequently, the file should be closed, the complaint having become without object. For these reasons,
The Competition Council
—	Receives the complaint; —	Notes that the case registered under number CONC-I/O-97/0016 has become without object; —	Consequently closes the file; Thus decided on 9 April 2002 by the chamber of the Competition Council composed of Mr Jacques Schaar, chamber president, Mrs Marie-Claude Grégoire, Mrs Dominique Smeets, and Mr David Szafran, members. FEDERAL PUBLIC SERVICE ECONOMY, SMEs, MIDDLE CLASSES AND ENERGY
[C − 2002/11434]
Competition Council. — Decision No. 2002-P/K-30 of 9 April 2002
Case CONC-PRA-95/0014 In the matter of:
Computer Media s.a., a company under Belgian law having its registered office at 97 rue Reper Vreven, 1020 Brussels, hereinafter referred to as the complainant. Against
A T&T Belgium s.a., a company under Belgian law having its registered office at 50 rue de la Fusée, 1130 Brussels. Having regard to the law on the protection of economic competition, coordinated on 1 July 1999; Having regard to the complaint filed on 6 July 1995 by the complainant with the Competition Service and registered on 11 July 1995 under number CONC-PRA-95/0014; Having regard to the report of the Body of rapporteurs of 30 January 2002; Having heard at the hearing of 9 April 2002, Mrs Anne Bouillet, for the body of rapporteurs, a hearing at which the complainant, although duly summoned, did not appear nor anyone on its behalf. Whereas the present procedure was initiated on 6 July 1995 pursuant to the law of 5 August 1991 on the protection of economic competition (hereinafter L.P.C.E.), which was amended by two laws of 26 April 1999 then coordinated by royal decree on 1 July 1999; that it should be noted that, since the initiation of the procedure, the complainant has not come forward again; Whereas Article 47 of the law of 26 April 1999 amending the law of 5 August 1991 on the protection of economic competition states that the provisions of the new law do not apply to procedures initiated with the Competition Council or the Brussels Court of Appeal at the time of entry into force of this law, namely 1 October 1999; Whereas the text concerning prescription has not been modified; Whereas the Competition Council considers that a procedure is pending before it from the moment it has been seized by the report of the Body of rapporteurs; Whereas in this case, the report of the Body of rapporteurs having been transmitted to the Competition Council on 30 January 2002, the amended law is applicable; Whereas Article 48, § 1, of the L.P.C.E. provides that the examination referred to in Article 23 may only relate to facts going back no more than five years. This period is counted from the date of the decision of the Competition Service to proceed with an examination on its own initiative or from the date of referral to the Service in accordance with Article 23,

### § 1

er."; that Article 48, § 2, of the L.P.C.E. provides that "The limitation period with respect to the investigation and decision procedure is five years from the date referred to in § 1. The limitation period shall only be interrupted by acts of investigation or decision made within the period determined in the preceding paragraph; these acts shall cause a new period of equal duration to run."
Whereas the Competition Council finds that no act of investigation has been made in this file for more than five years, that is, since the Competition Service, by facsimile dated 14 September 1995, invited the complainant to an information meeting to be held on 5 October 1995, a meeting of which no trace appears in the file; Whereas the limitation period provided for by Article 48, § 2, of the L.P.C.E. has therefore been reached; that the file should consequently be closed. For these reasons,
The Competition Council
— Receives the complaint
— Finds that the matter registered under number CONC-PRA-95/0014 is time-barred; — Consequently closes the file; Thus decided on 9 April 2002 by the chamber of the Competition Council composed of Mrs Marie-Claude Grégoire, chamber president, Mrs Dominique Smeets, Mr Jacques Schaar, and Mr David Szafran, members. *
FEDERAL PUBLIC SERVICE ECONOMY, SMEs, MIDDLE CLASSES AND ENERGY
[C − 2002/11447]
Competition Council. — Decision No. 2002-V/M-43 of 13 June 2002

Case CONC-V/M-02/0008: INTERDAMO S.A./ITM Belgium S.A. and Société centrale d'Approvisionnement en Produits régionaux S.A. I. The parties concerned
1. the applicant for interim measures
The applicant for interim measures is the S.A. under Belgian law INTERDAMO whose registered office is established at 4633 Melen, rue du Parc Hauzeur 70, registered in the commercial register of Verviers under number 68.141, which operates a supermarket-type business under the Intermarché sign. 2. The incriminated parties
2.1. S.A. ITM Belgium
The S.A. under Belgian law ITM Belgium, (hereinafter referred to as S.A. ITM or ITM) is registered in the commercial register of Nivelles under number 90.049 and has its registered office established at 1348 Louvain-la-Neuve, Traverse d'Esope 6. It represents the société anonyme under French law, ITM Entreprises (hereinafter S.A. ITME) of which it is a subsidiary and has as its object the establishment and promotion of a network of franchises for supermarket distribution under the Intermarché sign or related signs. 2.2. S.A. Société centrale d'Approvisionnement en Produits régionaux
The S.A. under Belgian law Société centrale d'Approvisionnement en Produits régionaux (hereinafter referred to as S.A. SCA) is registered in the commercial register of Namur under number 72.540 and has its registered office established at 5000 Beez, rue de Namur 45. This company, a subsidiary of ITM, is responsible for purchasing goods and supplying the franchises of the network ("Mousquetaires"). II. Background to the interim measures proceedings
S.A. Interdamo, by a complaint dated 14 January 2002 and filed on 18 January 2002, denounced a violation of Article 2 of the law of 5 August 1991 on the protection of economic competition against S.A. ITM Belgium and Société centrale d'Approvisionnement en produits régionaux and requested that the president of the Competition Council take, in accordance with Article 35 of that law, interim measures aimed at "suspending the rules in dispute". By a letter of 13 February 2002, the complaining party and applicant for interim measures indicated that it wished to file an amplifying complaint. The latter was filed on 20 February 2002. The Corps of Rapporteurs transmitted its reasoned report to the secretariat of the Competition Council on 28 February 2002. The hearing before the Competition Council was set for 21 March 2002. By letter of 18 March 2002, the applicant party for interim measures, through one of its counsel, informed the Competition Council that the president of the Commercial Court of Liège had appointed ex officio a provisional administrator for its client, having as first mission to summon it into bankruptcy and that under these conditions, it was to be feared that this action had become without interest. By letter of 20 March 2002, this lawyer informed the Competition Council that two provisional administrators had been appointed on 19 March 2002 by the president of the Commercial Court of Liège and requested an indefinite postponement of the interim measures hearing of 21 March 2002, to allow the provisional administrators time to become acquainted with the dispute and form an opinion on it. By fax of 20 March 2002, the Secretary of the Competition Council indicated, in accordance with the instructions given to him, that the hearing of 21 March was maintained and that if necessary, it was always possible to consider a continuation of the matter to allow the provisional administrators to express themselves or to appoint a representative for this purpose. On 21 March 2002, the examination of the matter was commenced in the presence of the Corps of Rapporteurs represented by
Mr P. Marchand, rapporteur and the Counsel of the incriminated parties. The applicant party being neither present nor represented, the matter was continued to 18 April 2002 to allow the appointed provisional administrators to intervene. By letter of 11 April 2002, one of the provisional administrators of S.A Interdamo indicated that he would not appear at the hearing of 18 April, the lawyer initially representing the company Interdamo being responsible for defending the position of this company. By fax of 18 April 2002, the latter indicated that S.A. Interdamo would not appear at the hearing of the same day and that in view of its situation and the fact that a summons into bankruptcy had been filed by the provisional administrators, it no longer intended to pursue the applications for interim measures which were without object. III. Facts of the case
The applicant for interim measures, S.A Interdamo operates a supermarket-type business mainly focused on food products, under the Intermarché sign. This company indeed signed on 1 July 1996 a sign contract (franchise) with S.A. ITM Belgium, exclusive holder in Belgian territory of the exploitation rights of the trademark relating to the Intermarché sign or related signs. The managing director of S.A. Interdamo, Dominique Langlet as well as his wife, Anne-Marie Langlet-Dejasse were co-signatories of this agreement. The spouses Langlet-Dejasse also signed with the S.A. under French law ITM Entreprises whose registered office is at 75015 Paris, rue Auguste Chabrières 24, a contract of adhesion to the "Mousquetaires Network" (on a date not specified in this agreement) through the intermediary of the S.A. under Belgian law ITM Belgium mandated for this purpose. These contracts were concluded for a period of ten years. From 1997, the parties mutually accused each other of not respecting the rules and principles contained in these agreements. It thus appears in particular from the documents transmitted by the applicant for interim measures, that from 26 March 1997, S.A. Interdamo had a bailiff's report made of the products ordered but not delivered by the company responsible for supplying this point of sale. The managing director of S.A. Interdamo maintains that this situation had already persisted for approximately two months. From the month of August 2000, numerous reproaches were formulated on both sides:
— S.A. ITM thus reproached in particular the spouses Langlet and S.A. Interdamo for a lack of spirit of solidarity and autonomous and too costly advertising campaigns; — S.A. Interdamo and the spouses Langlet for their part reproached S.A. SCA and S.A. ITM for a lack of punctuality in deliveries as well as deficiencies in the information they are required to transmit to them. In June 2001, Mrs A.-M. Dejasse appeared officially as managing director of S.A. Interdamo in place of her husband. In September 2001, Mr Langlet, majority shareholder of S.A. Interdamo informed S.A. ITM of his decision to reorient his professional career and to transfer his shares to his wife for a price of 36 million (BEF). In November 2001, S.A. ITM learned that Mr Langlet had allegedly been hired by a competing company, namely the company Carrefour. Mrs Dejasse confirmed this information and was prohibited on 28 November 2001 from participating in a coordination meeting at the headquarters of S.A. ITM. The interested party had this fact recorded by the bailiff Martine Cuisenaire. S.A. ITM, moreover, by letter of 5 December 2001, informed Mr Langlet that it was exercising the purchase option provided for in the adhesion contract while contesting the transfer price. According to the applicant party for interim measures, S.A. SCA allegedly modified from 29 November 2001 the order of rounds of its trucks departing from the Villers-le-Bouillet base in order to delay the delivery time of the point of sale that S.A. Interdamo operates in Battice. This had the effect, whereas it was usually delivered before 5:30 a.m., of being, from that date, supplied with perishable goods only shortly before 7 a.m. and sometimes even later (8:20 a.m. on 15 December 2001, 7:10 a.m. on 27 December 2001, 7:45 a.m. on 29 January 2002). Other failures relating to the communication of information necessary for operation or the supply of advertising furniture had also allegedly been committed according to S.A. Interdamo, at the end of 2001. By summons in summary proceedings of 18 December 2001, S.A. Interdamo asked the president of the commercial court of Verviers to order the suspension of the execution of the sign contract on the ground of deliberate failures by
S.A. ITM reflecting its intention to no longer collaborate loyally and in good faith with it. An order was rendered in the context of this action on 18 January 2002 and declared the applications formulated in the principal claim and in the subsidiary claim receivable but not well-founded. An appeal was lodged against this decision by
S.A. Interdamo. On 18 January 2002, S.A. Interdamo filed a complaint with the Competition Council and requested that interim measures be taken in accordance with Article 35 of the law of 5 August 1991 on the protection of economic competition. In view of the dispute between the parties concerning the ownership of the shares of S.A. Interdamo formerly held by Mr Langlet and the risks that these might be resold to a third party, S.A. ITM, on unilateral application, obtained on 7 February 2002 an order from the president of the Commercial Court of Liège, placing these shares under sequestration. By a judgment of 15 February 2002, the Court of Appeal of Liège confirmed the summary proceedings order of 18 January 2002. This judgment recognises the specificities of the Intermarché network and considers that the passage of Dominique Langlet to an important competitor (Carrefour), while he was the essential element of S.A. Interdamo, appeared as a direct violation of the adhesion contract and consequently a wrongful breach of the obligations contained in the sign contract. The wish of S.A. ITM to take back the shares of S.A. Interdamo of which Dominique Langlet was the holder is in line with what this contract provides. Moreover, this judgment refutes the various grievances advanced by S.A. Interdamo by finding that they are not based on a culpable breach, an abuse of right or bad faith on the part of S.A. ITM. On 19 February 2002, Interdamo filed an amplifying complaint with the Competition Council. By order of 19 March 2002, the president of the Commercial Court of Liège considered that the conditions for bankruptcy appeared to be met in the case of S.A. Interdamo and that there was urgency and absolute necessity to divest its organs of their management powers. In accordance with Article 8 of the law on bankruptcies of 8 August 1997, two provisional administrators were thus appointed with a view in particular to summoning this company into bankruptcy. By fax of 18 April 2002, one of the counsel of S.A. Interdamo indicated to the Competition Council that it no longer intended to pursue the applications for interim measures which were without object. IV. Grievances invoked by S.A. Interdamo
S.A Interdamo considers that the sign contracts and the adhesion contracts are contrary to Article 2 of the law of 5 August 1991 on the protection of economic competition given that they constitute an agreement between the different contracting parties (enterprises), which creates a network controlled by S.A. ITM for its sole and unique benefit, to the detriment of the franchises. It recalls that Article 2 of the law of 5 August 1991 on the protection of economic competition states that
"All agreements between enterprises, all decisions by associations of enterprises and all concerted practices which have as their object or effect the prevention, restriction or distortion in a significant manner of competition on the Belgian market concerned or in a substantial part thereof are prohibited and in particular those which consist in:
a) directly or indirectly fixing purchase or selling prices or other transaction conditions; (…)
d) applying unequal conditions to equivalent transactions with trading parties, thereby placing them at a competitive disadvantage; e) making the conclusion of contracts subject to acceptance by the parties of supplementary obligations which, by their nature or according to commercial usage, have no connection with the subject of these contracts."
S.A. Interdamo maintains that the adhesion agreements and the sign contracts signed by S.A. ITM Belgium with numerous affiliates contain numerous restrictions of competition which have no other purpose than to reduce the costs of S.A. ITM Belgium without however an equivalent consideration being granted to the franchises which moreover only experience negative balance sheets and results. These restrictive practices denounced by S.A. ITM can be summarised as follows:
1. Restriction of the freedom of action of adherents in advertising matters. Article 1.1., paragraph 2, of the sign contract stipulates that "The operating company undertakes to operate the business directly, without intermediary, tenant, or sub-concessionaire, at its full risk and for its entire benefit but respecting all its contractual obligations and the ITM system."
However, according to S.A. Interdamo, this provision is contradicted by Articles 4.4 and 5.6 of this contract and by the circumstance that, in fact, S.A. ITM refuses the franchises to do advertising that would better meet the expectations of the population that makes up their clientele and their catchment area. 2. Obligation to bear costs related to services performed on behalf of ITM or its subsidiaries. Article 5.8. of the sign contract provides in its paragraph 2 that the costs incurred by the adherent majority shareholder of the franchised company, during meetings organised by S.A. ITM Belgium, must be borne by this franchised company. S.A. Interdamo concludes that the purpose thus pursued is to reduce the operating costs of ITM to the detriment of the operating companies. 3. Systematic refusal of alternative suppliers. While Article 6 of the sign contract expressly provides that the operating company is free to obtain supplies from suppliers outside the network if it can provide proof that the quality-price ratio is more attractive, the applicant for interim measures affirms that, to do so, it is necessary to obtain the approval of
S.A. ITM and that this is systematically refused even when the franchises demonstrate that the selling price of a network product is higher than that practised by competitors. 4. Obligation to fix prices. The adhesion contract develops in its Chapter I, entitled "fundamental principles" a so-called low price policy. Paragraph 9 of this chapter specifies that "If the practice of low prices implies that it applies to all products, it must also be applied every day in all places by all members of the Network. (…) The maximum prices determined by the Network must (…) be applied by all (…)."
Chapter II indicates (that) "For this purpose, the supply or referencing companies of the Network publish indicative schedules of maximum selling prices calculated as precisely as possible. A single price higher than the indicated maximum price calls into question these basic principles."
SA Interdamo considers that the fixing of prices as provided for by these contracts is contrary to Article 2 of the law of 5 August 1991 on the protection of economic competition like what the Court of Justice of the European Communities and the European Commission decided in the Hennessy-Henkell case (O.J. No. L 383 of 31 December 1980). 5. Obligation of joint and several guarantee. The adhesion contract, states as second principle (paragraph 6), that "the Network is, above all, a gathering of business leaders, at the head of human-sized businesses … The person responsible for each business must be the majority contributor of capital and must engage his personal responsibility with regard to his obligations towards the Network or third parties…"
The applicant for interim measures concludes that the persons responsible for the franchised companies are required to respect the contractual conditions contrary to the law of 5 August 1991 on the protection of economic competition under penalty of sanctions. S.A. Interdamo further maintains that the majority of adherents experience difficult situations and are debtors vis-à-vis S.A. ITM Belgium and its subsidiaries including mainly S.A. SCA. This situation generates a threat of seizure of the personal property of the managers and conditions the behaviour of the franchised companies. 6. Interdependence. According to S.A. Interdamo, interdependence, the third principle of the adhesion contract, allows S.A. ITM and its parent company to indicate to the franchises that their precarious situation is insignificant compared to the future and the profit of the Network, that is, of ITME. The adherents and their companies must indeed favour the interests of the Network over their own interest. By this obligation included in the adhesion contract, the companies ITME and ITM Belgium manifestly forget, according to S.A. Interdamo, the foundations of economic freedom and free competition. According to the terms of S.A. Interdamo, examples of companies in difficulty abound in this group which would clearly demonstrate that the franchises are neglected to serve the financial interests of ITM. The franchises thus bear according to the applicant for interim measures, the economic, social and other costs of the poor policy of the Network justified by the interest of ITM. 7. "Third-Time" system. The adhesion contract provides in its Article 6 a rule called Third-Time, which the adherent undertakes to respect. It is in fact a matter for the adherents of devoting one third of their working time, (that is, two days per week), to the service of the Network, free of charge. The applicant for interim measures considers that this obligation has no other purpose than to give free labour to S.A. ITM and to allow it thus to reduce its fixed operating costs to the detriment of the franchises which must bear the costs of these services without the slightest consideration. 8. Mechanism sanctioning the transgression of contractual obligations. S.A. Interdamo also considers that the mechanism of sanctions provided for by the adhesion and sign contracts in case of breaches of contractual obligations, which leaves the application of these sanctions to the discretion of S.A. ITM, violates the rules of the law of 5 August 1991 on the protection of economic competition. 9. Mechanism relating to the transfer of shares of the franchised company
In its amplifying complaint filed on 20 February 2002, S.A. Interdamo invokes yet another new grievance resulting specifically from Article 5 of the adhesion contract which governs the transfer of shares of the franchised company and which grants a right of pre-emption to S.A. ITM. S.A. Interdamo considers that this right given to ITM can only be justified if the franchisor exercises it loyally, without resorting to mechanisms which have the purpose of reducing the advantages of the franchise for the sole benefit of the franchisor and of allowing the latter to obtain a point of sale at low price. V. Object of the application for interim measures
S.A. Interdamo requests that the president of the Competition Council suspend the rules in dispute, namely the adhesion and sign contracts, and impose penalty payments to encourage the incriminated parties to respect the decision to be rendered. At the very least, the applicant for interim measures requests the suspension of the adhesion contract especially its Articles 4, 5 and 10 as well as the corresponding provisions of the sign contract under penalty payments. VI. Markets concerned
The economic sector concerned by the present application is that of retail trade in non-specialised stores, with a predominance of food (NACE code 52.11). The market concerned relates to that of retail distribution of food products and non-food household articles of current consumption, aimed at satisfying the recurrent needs of households. In this regard, the Competition Council in its decision of 1 October 2001 No. 2001-C/C-53 "Delhaize De Leeuw - Supermarkt De Wolf" has already retained as the market concerned, the market of retail trade for current consumption goods by also referring to the case law of the European Commission in this matter which considers that there exists, in the distribution market, a distinct market which corresponds to retail distribution of food products and non-food household articles of current consumption, aimed at satisfying the recurrent needs of households. With regard to the geographical delimitation of the market concerned, the Competition Council considered in this same decision of 1 October 2001 No. 2001-C/C-53 "Delhaize De Leeuw - Supermarkt De Wolf", like the European Commission, that the geographical market relevant in the market of retail trade for current consumption goods, could cover either the entire territory, or one or more determined regions or a limited space. Neither the complainant, nor the Competition Service, nor the Corps of rapporteurs pronounced on the geographical market relevant in the context of this procedure. A document in the investigation file and filed by S.A. Interdamo allows it to be noted that the franchises of this network are mainly established in the Walloon region and to a lesser extent in the Flemish region. In the absence of a more in-depth examination on this point and given the fact on the one hand, that the applicant for interim measures seems informally to have renounced its application, and on the other hand, that the general economic interest does not appear to be threatened, there is no need to define the geographical market in question. VII. Arguments of the incriminated parties
S.A. ITM points out that the adhesion contract and the sign contract together establish a franchise between on the one hand the franchisor ITM and on the other hand the franchise adherents of its network. The conclusion of these contracts is carried out intuitu personae, that is to say in full "consideration of the personality of the adherent majority shareholder and manager of the operating company who, indeed, must possess professional experience, a capacity to adapt to the constraints of the life of the franchises of the ITM network, a certain number of indispensable moral qualities and a sufficient personal financial standing. S.A. ITM further considers that the dispute opposing it to S.A. Interdamo has its origin in the fact that Mr Langlet, by contravening this principle of intuitu personae, transferred in disregard of the contractual framework provided for this purpose, all of his shares in the company Interdamo to his wife and engaged with a competing large distribution chain of ITM, "Carrefour". When ITM wanted to exercise its right of pre-emption provided for in the adhesion contract, it found this right contested by the spouses Langlet. As for the legal regime of the franchise, based on the teachings of the Court of Justice, ITM affirms that all the classic clauses of the franchise contract, insofar as they are indispensable to protect the know-how of the franchisor on the one hand and to organise the control of the identity and reputation of the network on the other hand, are not considered as restrictive of competition within the meaning of Article 81, § 1, of the EC Treaty. S.A. ITM also specifies that it holds a market share of [0-5%] in Belgian territory. Consequently, the contracts entered into with its franchises are very largely below the threshold of 30% market share referred to in Community law by Regulation 2790/1999 on vertical restrictions. Therefore, according to S.A. ITM, they should benefit from the block exemption, unless it is established that they contain provisions set out in Article 4 of said regulation. Moreover, the contracts in question fall well below the 15% threshold defined by the Commission in its de minimis communication. As for the disputed clauses, S.A. ITM makes various observations:
- as for advertising: the provisions of the sign contract in no way prevent the franchise from programming its own promotional campaigns. The prior agreement of ITM stems from the right of the franchisor to control the commercial policy of the franchises in order to ensure that the promotional campaigns conducted individually do not harm the image and reputation of the network. Regulation 4087/88 on the franchise contract expressly authorised such practices in its Article 3.1, g). For the rest, given the fact that the contracts are exempt from territorial exclusivity, nothing prevents a franchise from conducting its own advertising campaign and canvassing clientele where it wishes. Competition between the franchises therefore plays freely. - the bearing of costs: by signing the contract, the adherent marks his adhesion to this principle which is again inherent in the very idea of a franchise contract (which groups several independents under a common sign). The restriction, purely contractual, cannot be qualified as a restriction of competition. - alternative suppliers: to develop a supply network, ITM has invested heavily to constitute a network of "affiliated companies" which offer specific services to the ITM network and achieve the conditions of quality and low prices. For reasons of profitability and balance of the organisation, ITM indicates to the franchises that said companies must be chosen by preference for their supply. In this regard, the Pronuptia and Gøttrup-Klim judgments recognise that such a clause must be admitted when it is necessary for the protection of the reputation of the network or when the objective pursued is not to jeopardise both the proper functioning of the cooperative and its contractual power vis-à-vis producers. Moreover, ITM does not prohibit its franchises from having other suppliers but asks them to prove that the product corresponds to the price and quality conditions specific to the network. In practice, adds ITM, all points of sale have their small "direct" suppliers for the supply of certain "proximity" products without this being the subject of censure. - fixing of maximum prices: the low price policy, which implies not exceeding the maximum prices set by the network, is also a characteristic of the ITM network. S.A. ITM specifies that the determination of maximum prices in vertical relations between enterprises does not constitute a restriction of competition prohibited by Article 81, § 1, EC. Competition plays for the fixing of lower and not higher prices. Consequently, maximum prices are considered as pro-competitive. The franchise is free to set its prices at the desired level, while respecting the maximum price, and therefore to compete with the other franchises within the network. The franchises agree not to make an excessive profit to the detriment of consumers. S.A. ITM further specified that in practice, the maximum prices are rather recommended prices which are not uniformly respected, without any sanction being taken. - joint and several guarantee: the philosophy of the franchise contract is to promote private initiative while allowing the franchises to benefit from the assets of a sign and know-how. As such, they commit themselves personally vis-à-vis the network and third parties. This is a contractual provision to which the franchise freely subscribed upon conclusion of the contract. - interdependence: S.A. ITM recalls that a franchise system is based by definition on the idea of interdependence between the franchises which share the same sign in order to preserve a certain harmony within the network which gives it its identity. ITM specifies that there is not in the principles of the adhesion contract, any restriction of competition which would go beyond what is indispensable to ensure the proper functioning of the franchise. - discretionary sanctions: S.A. ITM affirms that the sanctions referred to in the adhesion and sign contracts are in accordance with the right reserved to the franchisor to take the measures appropriate to preserve the identity and reputation of the network which is symbolised by the sign." In this sense, it is established that admissible are the obligations imposed on resellers to cooperate in the development of the network, and to conform to the commercial methods developed by the franchisor. This is not an infringement of Article 2 of the law, but rather a strictly contractual obligation which binds the signatory of the contract and which results in the termination of the latter when it is not respected
The S.A.ITM S.A. further points out that the subject matter of the present proceedings is if not identical, at least similar to the judicial proceedings that have been conducted between the parties. VIII. In law

For provisional measures within the meaning of Article 35 of the Act on the Protection of Economic Competition to be ordered by the President of the Competition Council, three conditions must be cumulatively fulfilled:

1. the existence of a complaint that is the subject of an investigation; 2. the prima facie existence of an infringement of the Act; 3. the probability of serious, imminent and irreparable harm to the applicant or of a nature to harm the general economic interest. 1. Existence of a complaint that is the subject of an investigation
